Yeah… most of the day, I’d say. Nearly every day too, if I’m being honest.

It’s not like I wake up and decide, “I’m not going to care about anything today.” It’s just there. Like I’ll pass my fishing gear in the garage—still sitting there, same as it’s been—and I used to be real particular about that stuff, you know, cleaning it, organizing the tackle box. Now I just look at it and keep walking.

Same with the woodshop. I used to lose track of time out there, and now it feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with. And the kids’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down with them, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. Moving pieces, pretending I’m more into it than I am.

So… yeah. It’s there most of the day. It doesn’t really let up much.

Like I’ll pass my fishing gear in the garage—still sitting there, same as it’s been—and I used to be real particular about that stuff, you know, cleaning it, organizing the tackle box. Now I just look at it and keep walking.\n\nSame with the woodshop. I used to lose track of time out there, and now it feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with. And the kids’ll ask me to play chess, and I’ll sit down with them, but I’m mostly just going through the motions. Moving pieces, pretending I’m more into it than I am.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s there most of the day. It doesn’t really let up much.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your symptoms are severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / construction_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve started missing things at work that could affect deadlines and safety, and after the near-miss I couldn’t pretend it was just stress anymore. At home I’m present physically, but my wife and kids are having to work around me instead of being able to count on me the way they used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it is. I mean, “severely” sounds like a big word, but… yeah, it’s gotten to that point.

At work I’ve started missing things I really shouldn’t miss. Not just, you know, forgetting to return a call or misplacing a form—stuff that can affect deadlines, and honestly, safety. In construction you can’t be half-there. One thing gets overlooked and suddenly everybody’s scrambling, or worse. After that near-miss, I couldn’t keep telling myself it was just normal stress.

And at home… I’m there, technically. I come in, I sit down, I’m around. But my wife and the kids are kind of having to work around me now, like I’m not someone they can lean on the same way. I used to be the guy who handled things, kept things moving. Lately I feel more like furniture in the room than a husband or a dad. So… yeah, it’s impairing things pretty badly.

And then sometimes I think about the truck, about just… steering off, and it scares me because it feels too close.\n\nThen I’ll get to the site and someone’s asking about concrete or permits or whatever, and I just shove it down because there’s always something needing an answer.
